**Clinton Eastwood Jr.** (born May 31, 1930) is an American actor, filmmaker and musician. After achieving success in the [Western](/wiki/Western_\(genre\) "Western \(genre\)") TV series _[Rawhide](/wiki/Rawhide_\(TV_series\) "Rawhide \(TV series\)")_ , Eastwood rose to international fame with his role as the "[Man with No Name](/wiki/Man_with_No_Name "Man with No Name")" in [Sergio Leone](/wiki/Sergio_Leone "Sergio Leone")'s _[Dollars Trilogy](/wiki/Dollars_Trilogy "Dollars Trilogy")_ of [spaghetti Westerns](/wiki/Spaghetti_Western "Spaghetti Western") during the mid-1960s and as [antihero](/wiki/Antihero "Antihero") cop [Harry Callahan](/wiki/Dirty_Harry_\(character\) "Dirty Harry \(character\)") in the five _[Dirty Harry](/wiki/Dirty_Harry_\(film_series\) "Dirty Harry \(film series\)")_ films throughout the 1970s and 1980s. These roles, among others, have made Eastwood an enduring [cultural icon](/wiki/Cultural_icon "Cultural icon") of [masculinity](/wiki/Masculinity "Masculinity").[4][5] Elected in 1986, Eastwood served for two years as the mayor of [Carmel-by-the-Sea, California](/wiki/Carmel-by-the-Sea,_California "Carmel-by-the-Sea, California"). 

Eastwood's greatest commercial successes are the adventure comedy _[Every Which Way but Loose](/wiki/Every_Which_Way_but_Loose "Every Which Way but Loose")_ (1978) and its action comedy sequel _[Any Which Way You Can](/wiki/Any_Which_Way_You_Can "Any Which Way You Can")_ (1980).[6] Other popular Eastwood films include the Westerns _[Hang 'Em High](/wiki/Hang_%27Em_High "Hang 'Em High")_ (1968), _[The Outlaw Josey Wales](/wiki/The_Outlaw_Josey_Wales "The Outlaw Josey Wales")_ (1976) and _[Pale Rider](/wiki/Pale_Rider "Pale Rider")_ (1985), the action-war film _[Where Eagles Dare](/wiki/Where_Eagles_Dare "Where Eagles Dare")_ (1968), the prison film _[Escape from Alcatraz](/wiki/Escape_from_Alcatraz_\(film\) "Escape from Alcatraz \(film\)")_ (1979), the war film _[Heartbreak Ridge](/wiki/Heartbreak_Ridge "Heartbreak Ridge")_ (1986), the action film _[In the Line of Fire](/wiki/In_the_Line_of_Fire "In the Line of Fire")_ (1993), and the romantic drama _[The Bridges of Madison County](/wiki/The_Bridges_of_Madison_County_\(film\) "The Bridges of Madison County \(film\)")_ (1995). More recent works include _[Gran Torino](/wiki/Gran_Torino "Gran Torino")_ (2008), _[The Mule](/wiki/The_Mule_\(2018_film\) "The Mule \(2018 film\)")_ (2018), and _[Cry Macho](/wiki/Cry_Macho_\(film\) "Cry Macho \(film\)")_ (2021). Since 1967, Eastwood's company [Malpaso Productions](/wiki/Malpaso_Productions "Malpaso Productions") has produced all but four of his American films. 

An [Academy Award](/wiki/Academy_Award "Academy Award") nominee for [Best Actor](/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Actor "Academy Award for Best Actor"), Eastwood won [Best Director](/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Director "Academy Award for Best Director") and [Best Picture](/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture "Academy Award for Best Picture") for his Western film _[Unforgiven](/wiki/Unforgiven "Unforgiven")_ (1992) and his sports drama _[Million Dollar Baby](/wiki/Million_Dollar_Baby "Million Dollar Baby")_ (2004). In addition to directing many of his own [star vehicles](/wiki/Star_vehicle "Star vehicle"), Eastwood has directed films in which he did not appear, such as the mystery drama _[Mystic River](/wiki/Mystic_River_\(film\) "Mystic River \(film\)")_ (2003) and the war film _[Letters from Iwo Jima](/wiki/Letters_from_Iwo_Jima "Letters from Iwo Jima")_ (2006), for which he received Academy Award nominations, as well as the legal thriller _[Juror #2](/wiki/Juror_No._2 "Juror No. 2")_ (2024). He also directed the biographical films _[Changeling](/wiki/Changeling_\(film\) "Changeling \(film\)")_ (2008), _[Invictus](/wiki/Invictus_\(film\) "Invictus \(film\)")_ (2009), _[American Sniper](/wiki/American_Sniper "American Sniper")_ (2014), _[Sully](/wiki/Sully_\(2016_film\) "Sully \(2016 film\)")_ (2016), and _[Richard Jewell](/wiki/Richard_Jewell_\(film\) "Richard Jewell \(film\)")_ (2019). 

[Eastwood's accolades](/wiki/List_of_awards_and_nominations_received_by_Clint_Eastwood "List of awards and nominations received by Clint Eastwood") include four Academy Awards, four [Golden Globe Awards](/wiki/Golden_Globe_Awards "Golden Globe Awards"), three [César Awards](/wiki/C%C3%A9sar_Awards "César Awards"), the [Golden Lion for Lifetime Achievement](/wiki/Golden_Lion_for_Lifetime_Achievement "Golden Lion for Lifetime Achievement"), the [Honorary Palme d'Or](/wiki/Honorary_Palme_d%27Or "Honorary Palme d'Or"), and an [AFI Life Achievement Award](/wiki/AFI_Life_Achievement_Award "AFI Life Achievement Award"). Bestowed two of France's highest civilian honors, he received the [Commander](/wiki/Commander "Commander") of the [Ordre des Arts et des Lettres](/wiki/Ordre_des_Arts_et_des_Lettres "Ordre des Arts et des Lettres") in 1994, and the [Legion of Honour](/wiki/Legion_of_Honour "Legion of Honour") in 2007. 

## Early life

Main article: [Early life and work of Clint Eastwood](/wiki/Early_life_and_work_of_Clint_Eastwood "Early life and work of Clint Eastwood")

Clinton Eastwood Jr. was born on May 31, 1930, at [Saint Francis Memorial Hospital](/wiki/Saint_Francis_Memorial_Hospital "Saint Francis Memorial Hospital") in San Francisco, to Ruth (née Margret[b] Runner; 1909–2006) and Clinton Eastwood (1906–1970), who at the time lived in Oakland's Lake Merritt neighborhood. During her son's fame, Ruth was known by the surname of her second husband, John Belden Wood (1913–2004), whom she married after the death of Clinton Sr.[9] Eastwood was nicknamed "Samson" by hospital nurses because he weighed 11 pounds 6 ounces (5.2 kg) at birth.[10][11] He has a younger sister, Jeanne Bernhardt (b. 1934).[12]

He is of English, Irish, Scottish, and Dutch ancestry.[13] Eastwood is descended from _[Mayflower](/wiki/Mayflower "Mayflower")_ passenger [William Bradford](/wiki/William_Bradford_\(governor\) "William Bradford \(governor\)"), making him the 12th generation of his family born in North America.[14][15][16] His family relocated three times during the 1930s as his father changed occupations.[17][18] Contrary to what Eastwood has suggested in media interviews, they did not move between 1940 and 1949.[19][20] After settling in [Piedmont, California](/wiki/Piedmont,_California "Piedmont, California"), the Eastwoods lived in an affluent part of town, had a swimming pool, belonged to a [country club](/wiki/Country_club "Country club"), and each parent drove their own car.[21] Eastwood's father worked as manufacturing executive at [Georgia-Pacific](/wiki/Georgia-Pacific "Georgia-Pacific") for most of his career.[22] As Clint and Jeanne grew older, Ruth took a clerical job at [IBM](/wiki/IBM "IBM").[23]

Eastwood attended [Piedmont Middle School](/wiki/Piedmont_Middle_School "Piedmont Middle School"),[24] where he was held back due to poor academic performance, and records indicate he also attended summer school.[19] From January 1945 until at least January 1946, he attended [Piedmont High School](/wiki/Piedmont_High_School_\(California\) "Piedmont High School \(California\)"), but was asked to leave after writing an obscene suggestion to a school official on the athletic field scoreboard and burning an [effigy](/wiki/Effigy "Effigy") on the school lawn, in addition to other infractions.[25] He transferred to [Oakland Technical High School](/wiki/Oakland_Technical_High_School "Oakland Technical High School") and graduated on February 2, 1949.[26]

Eastwood worked a number of odd jobs, including lifeguard, paper carrier, grocery clerk, forest firefighter, and golf caddy.[27] He said he attempted to enroll at [Seattle University](/wiki/Seattle_University "Seattle University") in 1951,[28] but instead was drafted into the [United States Army](/wiki/United_States_Army "United States Army") during the [Korean War](/wiki/Korean_War "Korean War").[29] Don Loomis recalled hearing that Eastwood was romancing one of the daughters of a Fort Ord officer, who may have been encouraged to look out for him when postings were assigned.[30] While returning from a prearranged tryst[30] in Seattle, he was a passenger on a [Douglas AD bomber](/wiki/Douglas_A-1_Skyraider "Douglas A-1 Skyraider") that ran out of fuel and crashed into the ocean near [Point Reyes](/wiki/Point_Reyes "Point Reyes").[31][32] Using a life raft, he and the pilot swam 2 miles (3.2 km) to safety.[33] Eastwood was discharged in February 1953.[34]

## Career

### 1954–1962: Acting debut and _Rawhide_

[](/wiki/File:Clint_Eastwood_Nina_Foch_Rawhide_1959.jpg)With [Nina Foch](/wiki/Nina_Foch "Nina Foch") in an episode of _Rawhide_ , 1959

According to a CBS press release for _[Rawhide](/wiki/Rawhide_\(TV_series\) "Rawhide \(TV series\)")_ , [Universal-International](/wiki/Universal_Pictures "Universal Pictures")'s camera crew was shooting in Fort Ord when an enterprising assistant spotted Eastwood and invited him to meet the director,[35] although this is disputed by Eastwood's unauthorized biographer, Patrick McGilligan.[36] According to Eastwood's official biography, the key figure was a man named Chuck Hill, who was stationed in Fort Ord and had contacts in Hollywood.[35] While in Los Angeles, Hill became reacquainted with Eastwood and managed to sneak him into a Universal studio, where he introduced him to cameraman [Irving Glassberg](/wiki/Irving_Glassberg "Irving Glassberg").[35] Glassberg arranged for an audition under [Arthur Lubin](/wiki/Arthur_Lubin "Arthur Lubin"), who, although very impressed with Eastwood's appearance and stature (then 6 ft 4 in [193 cm]), disapproved of his acting, remarking, "He was quite amateurish. He didn't know which way to turn or which way to go or do anything."[37] Lubin suggested that he attend drama classes and arranged for Eastwood's initial contract in April 1954, at $100 per week.[37] After signing, Eastwood was initially criticized for his stiff manner and delivering his lines through his teeth, a lifelong trademark.[38]

In May 1954, Eastwood made his first real audition for _[Six Bridges to Cross](/wiki/Six_Bridges_to_Cross "Six Bridges to Cross")_ , but was rejected by [Joseph Pevney](/wiki/Joseph_Pevney "Joseph Pevney").[39] After many unsuccessful auditions, he was eventually given a minor role by director [Jack Arnold](/wiki/Jack_Arnold_\(director\) "Jack Arnold \(director\)") in _[Revenge of the Creature](/wiki/Revenge_of_the_Creature "Revenge of the Creature")_ (1955), a sequel to the recently released _[Creature from the Black Lagoon](/wiki/Creature_from_the_Black_Lagoon "Creature from the Black Lagoon")_.[40] In September 1954, Eastwood worked for three weeks on Arthur Lubin's _[Lady Godiva of Coventry](/wiki/Lady_Godiva_of_Coventry "Lady Godiva of Coventry")_ , won a role in February 1955, playing "Jonesy", a sailor in _[Francis in the Navy](/wiki/Francis_in_the_Navy "Francis in the Navy")_ and appeared uncredited in another Jack Arnold film, _[Tarantula](/wiki/Tarantula_\(film\) "Tarantula \(film\)")_ , where he played a squadron pilot.[41][42] In May 1955, Eastwood put four hours' work into the film _[Never Say Goodbye](/wiki/Never_Say_Goodbye_\(1956_film\) "Never Say Goodbye \(1956 film\)")_ and had a minor uncredited role as a ranch hand in August 1955 with _[Star in the Dust](/wiki/Star_in_the_Dust "Star in the Dust")_ , starring [John Agar](/wiki/John_Agar "John Agar") and [Mamie Van Doren](/wiki/Mamie_Van_Doren "Mamie Van Doren"), the latter of whom he dated briefly.[43] Universal presented him with his first television role on July 2, 1955, on [NBC](/wiki/NBC "NBC")'s _Allen in Movieland_ , which starred comedian [Steve Allen](/wiki/Steve_Allen "Steve Allen"), actor [Tony Curtis](/wiki/Tony_Curtis "Tony Curtis"), and swing musician [Benny Goodman](/wiki/Benny_Goodman "Benny Goodman").[44] Although he continued to develop as an actor, Universal terminated his contract on October 23, 1955.[45]

Eastwood joined the Marsh Agency, and although Lubin landed him his biggest role to date in _[The First Traveling Saleslady](/wiki/The_First_Traveling_Saleslady "The First Traveling Saleslady")_ (1956) and later hired him for _[Escapade in Japan](/wiki/Escapade_in_Japan "Escapade in Japan")_ (1957), without a formal contract, Eastwood was struggling.[46] On his financial advisor [Irving Leonard](/wiki/Irving_Leonard_\(financial_adviser\) "Irving Leonard \(financial adviser\)")'s advice, he switched to the Kumin-Olenick Agency in 1956 and Mitchell Gertz in 1957. He landed several small roles in 1956 as a temperamental army officer for a segment of [ABC](/wiki/American_Broadcasting_Company "American Broadcasting Company")'s _Reader's Digest_ series, and as a motorcycle gang member on a _[Highway Patrol](/wiki/Highway_Patrol_\(American_TV_series\) "Highway Patrol \(American TV series\)")_ episode.[46] In 1957, Eastwood played a cadet in the _[West Point](/wiki/The_West_Point_Story_\(TV_series\) "The West Point Story \(TV series\)")_ series and a suicidal gold prospector on _[Death Valley Days](/wiki/Death_Valley_Days "Death Valley Days")_.[47]

In 1958, he played a Navy lieutenant in a segment of _[Navy Log](/wiki/Navy_Log "Navy Log")_ and in early 1959 made a notable guest appearance as Red Hardigan on _[Maverick](/wiki/Maverick_\(TV_series\) "Maverick \(TV series\)")_ opposite [James Garner](/wiki/James_Garner "James Garner") as a cowardly villain intent on marrying a rich girl for money.[47] Eastwood had a small part as an aviator in _[Lafayette Escadrille](/wiki/Lafayette_Escadrille_\(film\) "Lafayette Escadrille \(film\)")_ (1958) and played a major role as an ex-renegade of the [Confederacy](/wiki/Confederate_States_of_America "Confederate States of America") in _[Ambush at Cimarron Pass](/wiki/Ambush_at_Cimarron_Pass "Ambush at Cimarron Pass")_ (also 1958), a film that Eastwood considers the low point of his career.[48][49][50]

[](/wiki/File:Clint_Eastwood_and_Roxanne_Tunis.jpg)With stuntwoman Roxanne Tunis on the _Rawhide_ set, circa 1959

In 1958, Eastwood was cast as Rowdy Yates in the CBS hour-long western series _[Rawhide](/wiki/Rawhide_\(TV_series\) "Rawhide \(TV series\)")_ , the career breakthrough he had long sought.[51][52] Eastwood was not especially happy with his character; Eastwood was almost 30, and Rowdy was too young and cloddish for his comfort.[53] Filming began in Arizona in the summer of 1958.[54] It took just three weeks for _Rawhide_ to reach the top 20 in TV ratings and, although it never won an [Emmy](/wiki/Emmy "Emmy"), it was a major success for several years, and peaked at number six in the ratings from October 1960 to April 1961.[55] The _Rawhide_ years (1959–65) were some of the most grueling of Eastwood's career, often filming six days a week for an average of 12 hours a day, but some directors still criticized him for not working hard enough.[55][56] By late 1963, _Rawhide_ was beginning to decline in the ratings and lacked freshness in the scripts; it was canceled in the middle of the 1965–66 season.[57] Eastwood made his first attempt at directing when he filmed several trailers for the show, but was unable to convince producers to let him direct an episode.[58] In the show's first season, Eastwood earned $750 an episode. At the time of _Rawhide_ 's cancellation, he received $119,000 an episode as severance pay.[59]

### 1963–1969: Spaghetti Westerns and stardom

In late 1963, Eastwood's _Rawhide_ co-star [Eric Fleming](/wiki/Eric_Fleming "Eric Fleming") rejected an offer to star in an Italian-made western called _[A Fistful of Dollars](/wiki/A_Fistful_of_Dollars "A Fistful of Dollars")_ (1964), filmed in a remote region of Spain by a then–relatively unknown director, [Sergio Leone](/wiki/Sergio_Leone "Sergio Leone").[60] [Richard Harrison](/wiki/Richard_Harrison_\(actor\) "Richard Harrison \(actor\)") suggested Eastwood to Leone because Harrison knew that Eastwood could play a cowboy convincingly. Eastwood thought the film would be an opportunity to escape from his _Rawhide_ image. He signed a contract for $15,000 in wages for eleven weeks' work, with a bonus of a [Mercedes-Benz](/wiki/Mercedes-Benz "Mercedes-Benz") automobile upon completion.[61][62] Eastwood later said of the transition from a TV western to _A Fistful of Dollars_ : "In _Rawhide_ I did get awfully tired of playing the conventional white hat. The hero who kisses old ladies and dogs and was kind to everybody. I decided it was time to be an [antihero](/wiki/Antihero "Antihero")."[63] Eastwood was instrumental in creating the [Man with No Name](/wiki/Man_with_No_Name "Man with No Name") character's distinctive visual style and, although a nonsmoker, Leone insisted Eastwood smoke cigars as an essential ingredient of the "mask" he was attempting to create for the character.[64] "I needed a mask more than an actor," Leone would later explain, "and back then Eastwood had only two facial expressions: with the hat and without the hat".[65]

_A Fistful of Dollars_ proved a landmark in the development of [spaghetti Westerns](/wiki/Spaghetti_Western "Spaghetti Western"), with Leone depicting a more lawless and desolate world than traditional westerns, and challenging the American stereotype of the western herο by offering a morally ambiguous antihero. The film's success made Eastwood a major star in Italy[66] and he was rehired to star in _[For a Few Dollars More](/wiki/For_a_Few_Dollars_More "For a Few Dollars More")_ (1965), the second of the trilogy. Through the efforts of screenwriter [Luciano Vincenzoni](/wiki/Luciano_Vincenzoni "Luciano Vincenzoni"), the rights to _For a Few Dollars More_ and the trilogy's final film, _[The Good, the Bad and the Ugly](/wiki/The_Good,_the_Bad_and_the_Ugly "The Good, the Bad and the Ugly")_ (1966), were sold to [United Artists](/wiki/United_Artists "United Artists") for about $900,000.[67]

In January 1966, Eastwood met producer [Dino De Laurentiis](/wiki/Dino_De_Laurentiis "Dino De Laurentiis") in New York City and agreed to star in a non-Western five-part anthology production, _[The Witches](/wiki/The_Witches_\(1967_film\) "The Witches \(1967 film\)")_ (_Le Streghe_ , 1967), opposite De Laurentiis's wife, [Silvana Mangano](/wiki/Silvana_Mangano "Silvana Mangano").[68] Eastwood's 19-minute installment took only a few days to shoot. Two months later he began work on _The Good, the Bad and the Ugly_ , again playing the mysterious Man with No Name. [Lee Van Cleef](/wiki/Lee_Van_Cleef "Lee Van Cleef") returned as a ruthless fortune seeker, with [Eli Wallach](/wiki/Eli_Wallach "Eli Wallach") portraying the Mexican bandit Tuco Ramirez. The storyline involved the search for a cache of [Confederate gold](/wiki/Confederate_gold "Confederate gold") buried in a cemetery. During the filming of a scene in which a bridge was blown up, Eastwood urged Wallach to retreat to a hilltop. "I know about these things", he said. "Stay as far away from special effects and explosives as you can."[69] Minutes later, confusion among the crew over the word "Vaya!" resulted in a premature explosion that could have killed Wallach.[69]

> I wanted to play it with an economy of words and create this whole feeling through attitude and movement. It was just the kind of character I had envisioned for a long time, keep to the mystery and allude to what happened in the past. It came about after the frustration of doing _Rawhide_ for so long. I felt the less he said, the stronger he became and the more he grew in the imagination of the audience.

— Eastwood, on playing the Man with No Name character[70]

[](/wiki/File:Clint_Eastwood_-_1960s.JPG)As the Man with No Name in _A Fistful of Dollars_ (1964)

The _Dollars_ trilogy was not released in the United States until 1967, when _A Fistful of Dollars_ opened on January 18, followed by _For a Few Dollars More_ on May 10, and _The Good, the Bad and the Ugly_ on December 29.[71] All three were commercially successful, particularly _The Good, the Bad and the Ugly_ , which eventually earned $8 million in rental earnings and turned Eastwood into a major film star being ranked for the first time on [Quigley's](/wiki/Quigley_Publishing_Company "Quigley Publishing Company") [Top Ten Money Making Stars Poll](/wiki/Top_Ten_Money_Making_Stars_Poll "Top Ten Money Making Stars Poll") in 1968 in fifth place.[71][72] All three received poor reviews, and marked the beginning of a battle for Eastwood to win American film critics' respect.[73] [Judith Crist](/wiki/Judith_Crist "Judith Crist") described _A Fistful of Dollars_ as "cheapjack",[74] while _[Newsweek](/wiki/Newsweek "Newsweek")_ called _For a Few Dollars More_ "excruciatingly dopey". [Renata Adler](/wiki/Renata_Adler "Renata Adler") of _[The New York Times](/wiki/The_New_York_Times "The New York Times")_ said _The Good, the Bad and the Ugly_ was "the most expensive, pious and repellent movie in the history of its peculiar genre".[75] _[Time](/wiki/Time_\(magazine\) "Time \(magazine\)")_ drew attention to the film's wooden acting, especially Eastwood's, though a few critics such as [Vincent Canby](/wiki/Vincent_Canby "Vincent Canby") and [Bosley Crowther](/wiki/Bosley_Crowther "Bosley Crowther") of _The New York Times_ praised his coolness.[76] Leone's cinematography was widely acclaimed, even by critics who disparaged the acting.[73]

Stardom brought Eastwood more roles. He signed to star in the American [revisionist](/wiki/Revisionism_\(fictional\) "Revisionism \(fictional\)") western _[Hang 'Em High](/wiki/Hang_%27Em_High "Hang 'Em High")_ (1968) alongside [Inger Stevens](/wiki/Inger_Stevens "Inger Stevens"), [Pat Hingle](/wiki/Pat_Hingle "Pat Hingle"), [Ed Begley](/wiki/Ed_Begley "Ed Begley"),[77] playing a man who takes up a marshal's badge and seeks revenge as a lawman after being lynched by [vigilantes](/wiki/Vigilantes "Vigilantes") and left for dead.[78] The film earned Eastwood $400,000 and 25% of its net box office.[77] Using money earned from the _Dollars_ trilogy, Eastwood's advisor Irving Leonard helped establish Eastwood's own production company, [Malpaso Productions](/wiki/Malpaso_Productions "Malpaso Productions"), named after [Malpaso Creek](/wiki/Malpaso_Creek "Malpaso Creek") on Eastwood's property in [Monterey County, California](/wiki/Monterey_County,_California "Monterey County, California"). The 38-year-old actor was still relatively unknown as late as a month prior to the film's release, as evidenced by a July 1968 news item by syndicated columnist Dorothy Manners: "The proverbial man in the street is still asking, 'Who's Clint Eastwood?'"[79] Leonard arranged for _Hang 'Em High_ to be a joint production with United Artists;[80] when it opened in August, it had the largest opening weekend in United Artists' history. _Hang 'Em High_ was widely praised by critics, including Archer Winsten of the _[New York Post](/wiki/New_York_Post "New York Post")_ , who called it "a western of quality, courage, danger and excitement".[16]

Before _Hang 'Em High_ 's release, Eastwood had already begun working on _[Coogan's Bluff](/wiki/Coogan%27s_Bluff_\(film\) "Coogan's Bluff \(film\)")_ (1968), about an Arizona deputy sheriff tracking a wanted psychopathic criminal ([Don Stroud](/wiki/Don_Stroud "Don Stroud")) through New York City. He was reunited with Universal Studios for it after receiving an offer of $1 million - more than double his previous salary.[81] [Jennings Lang](/wiki/Jennings_Lang "Jennings Lang") arranged for Eastwood to meet [Don Siegel](/wiki/Don_Siegel "Don Siegel"), a Universal contract director who later became Eastwood's close friend, forming a partnership that would last more than ten years and produce five films.[82] Shooting began in November 1967, before the script had been finalized.[83] The film was controversial for its portrayal of violence.[84][85] _Coogan's Bluff_ also became the first collaboration with Argentine composer [Lalo Schifrin](/wiki/Lalo_Schifrin "Lalo Schifrin"), who scored several Eastwood films in the 1970s and 1980s, including the _Dirty Harry_ films.[86]

Eastwood was paid $750,000 for the war epic _[Where Eagles Dare](/wiki/Where_Eagles_Dare "Where Eagles Dare")_ (1968),[87] about a World War II squad parachuting into a [Gestapo](/wiki/Gestapo "Gestapo") stronghold in [the Alps](/wiki/The_Alps "The Alps"). [Richard Burton](/wiki/Richard_Burton "Richard Burton") played the squad's commander, with Eastwood as his right-hand man. Eastwood was also cast as [Two-Face](/wiki/Two-Face "Two-Face") in the _[Batman](/wiki/Batman_\(TV_series\) "Batman \(TV series\)")_ television show, but the series was canceled before filming began.[88]

Eastwood then branched out to co-star in a musical, _[Paint Your Wagon](/wiki/Paint_Your_Wagon_\(film\) "Paint Your Wagon \(film\)")_ (1969). Eastwood and [Lee Marvin](/wiki/Lee_Marvin "Lee Marvin") play gold miners who buy a Mormon settler's less favored wife ([Jean Seberg](/wiki/Jean_Seberg "Jean Seberg")) at an auction. Bad weather and delays plagued the production, and the film's budget eventually exceeded $20 million, which was high for the time.[89] The film was not a critical or commercial success, but was nominated for a [Golden Globe Award for Best Motion Picture – Musical or Comedy](/wiki/Golden_Globe_Award_for_Best_Motion_Picture_%E2%80%93_Musical_or_Comedy "Golden Globe Award for Best Motion Picture – Musical or Comedy").[90]

### 1970–1989: Directorial debut and _Dirty Harry_

Eastwood starred with [Shirley MacLaine](/wiki/Shirley_MacLaine "Shirley MacLaine") in the western _[Two Mules for Sister Sara](/wiki/Two_Mules_for_Sister_Sara "Two Mules for Sister Sara")_ (1970), directed by Don Siegel. The film follows an American mercenary, who becomes mixed up with a prostitute disguised as a nun, and ends up helping a group of [Juarista](/wiki/Juarista "Juarista") rebels during the reign of Emperor [Maximilian I of Mexico](/wiki/Maximilian_I_of_Mexico "Maximilian I of Mexico").[91][92] Eastwood again played a mysterious stranger - unshaven, wearing a serape-like vest, and smoking a cigar.[93] Although it received moderate reviews,[94][95][96] the film is listed in _The New York Times Guide to the Best 1,000 Movies Ever Made_.[97] Around the same time, Eastwood starred as one of a group of Americans who steals a fortune in gold from the Nazis, in the World War II film _[Kelly's Heroes](/wiki/Kelly%27s_Heroes "Kelly's Heroes")_ (also 1970), with [Donald Sutherland](/wiki/Donald_Sutherland "Donald Sutherland") and [Telly Savalas](/wiki/Telly_Savalas "Telly Savalas"). _Kelly's Heroes_ was the last film Eastwood appeared in that was not produced by his own Malpaso Productions.[98] Shot on location in Yugoslavia and London,[99] the film received mostly a positive reception and its anti-war sentiments were recognized.[98] Siegel directed Eastwood's next film, _[The Beguiled](/wiki/The_Beguiled_\(1971_film\) "The Beguiled \(1971 film\)")_ (1971), a tale of a wounded Union soldier, held captive by the sexually repressed matron (played by [Geraldine Page](/wiki/Geraldine_Page "Geraldine Page")) of a Southern girls' school.[100] Upon release the film received major recognition in France and is considered one of Eastwood's finest works by French critics.[101] However, it grossed less than $1 million and, according to Eastwood and Lang, flopped due to poor publicity and the "emasculated" role of Eastwood.[102]

[](/wiki/File:PL_Wwa,_sztuka_w_miescie,_mural,_ul_Nowolipki,_2022.09.02,_fot_Ivonna_Nowicka_\(4\)_corr.jpg)A mural in Warsaw, Poland, referring to Eastwood's Dirty Harry character with a quote in English and [Esperanto](/wiki/Esperanto "Esperanto")

Eastwood's career reached a turning point in 1971.[103] Before Irving Leonard died, he and Eastwood had discussed the idea of Malpaso producing _[Play Misty for Me](/wiki/Play_Misty_for_Me "Play Misty for Me")_ , a film that was to give Eastwood the artistic control he desired, and his debut as a director.[104] The script was about a jazz disc jockey named Dave (Eastwood), who has a casual affair with Evelyn ([Jessica Walter](/wiki/Jessica_Walter "Jessica Walter")), a listener who had been calling the radio station repeatedly at night, asking him to play her favorite song – [Erroll Garner](/wiki/Erroll_Garner "Erroll Garner")'s "[Misty](/wiki/Misty_\(song\) "Misty \(song\)")". When Dave ends their relationship, the unhinged Evelyn becomes a murderous stalker.[105] Filming commenced in [Monterey](/wiki/Monterey "Monterey") in September 1970 and included footage of that year's [Monterey Jazz Festival](/wiki/Monterey_Jazz_Festival "Monterey Jazz Festival").[106] The film was highly acclaimed with critics, such as [Jay Cocks](/wiki/Jay_Cocks "Jay Cocks") in _Time_ , [Andrew Sarris](/wiki/Andrew_Sarris "Andrew Sarris") in the _[Village Voice](/wiki/Village_Voice "Village Voice")_ , and Archer Winsten in the _New York Post_ all praising the film, as well as Eastwood's directorial skills and performance.[107] Walter was nominated for a Golden Globe Best Actress Award (Drama), for her performance in the film.[108]

> I know what you're thinking - "Did he fire six shots or only five?" Well, to tell you the truth, in all this excitement, I've kinda lost track myself. But, being as this is a .44 Magnum, the most powerful handgun in the world and would blow your head clean off, you've got to ask yourself one question: "Do I feel lucky?" Well, do you, punk?

— Eastwood, in _Dirty Harry_

_[Dirty Harry](/wiki/Dirty_Harry "Dirty Harry")_ (1971), written by [Harry](/wiki/Harry_Julian_Fink "Harry Julian Fink") and [Rita Fink](/wiki/Rita_Fink "Rita Fink"), centers on a hard-edged New York City (later changed to San Francisco) [police inspector](/wiki/Police_inspector#Municipal_police "Police inspector") named [Harry Callahan](/wiki/Harry_Callahan_\(character\) "Harry Callahan \(character\)") who is determined to stop a psychotic killer by any means.[109] _Dirty Harry_ has been described as being arguably Eastwood's most memorable character, and the film has been credited with inventing the "loose-cannon cop" genre.[110][111] Author Eric Lichtenfeld argues that Eastwood's role as Dirty Harry established the "first true archetype" of the action film genre.[112] His lines (quoted above) are regarded by firearms historians, such as Garry James and Richard Venola, as the force that catapulted the ownership of [.44 Magnum](/wiki/.44_Magnum ".44 Magnum") revolvers to new heights in the United States; specifically the [Smith & Wesson Model 29](/wiki/Smith_%26_Wesson_Model_29 "Smith & Wesson Model 29") carried by Harry Callahan.[113][114] _Dirty Harry_ , released in December 1971, earned $22 million in the United States and Canada.[115] It was Siegel's highest-grossing film and the start of a series of films featuring the character Harry Callahan. Although a number of critics praised Eastwood's performance as Dirty Harry, such as Jay Cocks who described him as "giving his best performance so far, tense, tough, full of implicit identification with his character",[116] the film was also widely criticized as being [fascistic](/wiki/Fascism "Fascism").[117][118][119] After having been second for the past two years, Eastwood was voted first in Quigley's Top Ten Money Making Stars Poll in 1972 and again in 1973.[120]

Following [Sean Connery](/wiki/Sean_Connery "Sean Connery")'s announcement that he would not play [James Bond](/wiki/James_Bond "James Bond") again, Eastwood was offered the role but turned it down, saying, "that was someone else's gig. That's Sean's deal. It didn't feel right for me to be doing it."[121] He next starred in the loner Western _[Joe Kidd](/wiki/Joe_Kidd "Joe Kidd")_ (1972), based on a character inspired by [Reies Lopez Tijerina](/wiki/Reies_Lopez_Tijerina "Reies Lopez Tijerina"), who stormed a courthouse in [Tierra Amarilla](/wiki/Tierra_Amarilla,_New_Mexico "Tierra Amarilla, New Mexico"), New Mexico, in June 1967. During filming, Eastwood suffered symptoms of a bronchial infection and several panic attacks.[122] _Joe Kidd_ received a mixed reception, with [Roger Greenspun](/wiki/Roger_Greenspun "Roger Greenspun") of _The New York Times_ writing that it was unremarkable, with foolish symbolism and sloppy editing, although he praised Eastwood's performance.[123]

Eastwood's first western as director was _[High Plains Drifter](/wiki/High_Plains_Drifter "High Plains Drifter")_ (1973), in which he also starred. The film had a moral and supernatural theme, later emulated in _Pale Rider_. The plot follows a mysterious stranger (Eastwood) who arrives in a brooding Western town where the people hire him to protect them against three soon-to-be-released felons. There remains confusion during the film as to whether the stranger is the brother of the deputy, whom the felons lynched and murdered, or his ghost. Holes in the plot were filled with black humor and allegory, influenced by Leone.[124] The revisionist film received a mixed reception, but was a major box-office success. A number of critics thought Eastwood's directing was "as derivative as it was expressive", with [Arthur Knight](/wiki/Arthur_Knight_\(film_critic\) "Arthur Knight \(film critic\)") of the _[Saturday Review](/wiki/Saturday_Review_\(US_magazine\) "Saturday Review \(US magazine\)")_ remarking that Eastwood had "absorbed the approaches of Siegel and Leone and fused them with his own paranoid vision of society".[125] [John Wayne](/wiki/John_Wayne "John Wayne"), who had declined a role in the film, sent a letter to Eastwood soon after the film's release in which he complained that "The townspeople did not represent the true spirit of the American pioneer, the spirit that made America great."[126]

[](/wiki/File:Clint_Eastwood-Holden-Breezy.jpg)Directing William Holden in _Breezy_ , 1972

Eastwood next turned his attention towards _[Breezy](/wiki/Breezy "Breezy")_ (1973), a film starring [William Holden](/wiki/William_Holden "William Holden") and [Kay Lenz](/wiki/Kay_Lenz "Kay Lenz") about love blossoming between a middle-aged man and a teenage girl. Various actresses had lobbied for the title role, including Eastwood's future longtime companion [Sondra Locke](/wiki/Sondra_Locke "Sondra Locke"), who at 29 was nearly twice the character's age.[c] The film, shot very quickly and efficiently by [Frank Stanley](/wiki/Frank_Stanley_\(cinematographer\) "Frank Stanley \(cinematographer\)"), came in $1 million under budget and was finished three days ahead of schedule.[131] _Breezy_ was not a major critical or commercial success.[9] Eastwood then stepped back in front of the camera to reprise his role as Harry Callahan in _[Magnum Force](/wiki/Magnum_Force "Magnum Force")_ (1973), a sequel to _Dirty Harry_ , about a group of rogue young officers (among them [David Soul](/wiki/David_Soul "David Soul"), [Robert Urich](/wiki/Robert_Urich "Robert Urich"), and [Tim Matheson](/wiki/Tim_Matheson "Tim Matheson")) in the San Francisco Police Department who systematically exterminate the city's worst criminals.[132] Although the film was a major success after release, grossing $58.1 million in the United States (a record for Eastwood), it was not a critical success.[133][134] _The New York Times_ critic [Nora Sayre](/wiki/Nora_Sayre "Nora Sayre") panned the often contradictory moral themes of the film, while the paper's [Frank Rich](/wiki/Frank_Rich "Frank Rich") called it "the same old stuff".[134]

Eastwood teamed up with [Jeff Bridges](/wiki/Jeff_Bridges "Jeff Bridges") and [George Kennedy](/wiki/George_Kennedy "George Kennedy") in the buddy action caper _[Thunderbolt and Lightfoot](/wiki/Thunderbolt_and_Lightfoot "Thunderbolt and Lightfoot")_ (1974), a road movie about a veteran bank robber Thunderbolt (Eastwood) and a young con man drifter, Lightfoot (Bridges). On its release in spring 1974, the film was praised for its offbeat comedy mixed with high suspense and tragedy—but was only a modest success at the box office, earning $32.4 million.[135] Eastwood's acting was noted by critics, but was overshadowed by Bridges, who was nominated for an [Academy Award for Best Supporting Actor](/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Supporting_Actor "Academy Award for Best Supporting Actor"). Eastwood reportedly fumed at the lack of Academy Award recognition for him and swore that he would never work for United Artists again.[135][136]

[](/wiki/File:Film_shoot_for_The_Eiger_Sanction_in_Zurich_\(10\).jpg)With an unidentified colleague at a film shoot for _The Eiger Sanction_ , 1974

Eastwood's next film _[The Eiger Sanction](/wiki/The_Eiger_Sanction_\(film\) "The Eiger Sanction \(film\)")_ (1975) was based on [Trevanian](/wiki/Trevanian "Trevanian")'s [critically acclaimed spy novel](/wiki/The_Eiger_Sanction_\(novel\) "The Eiger Sanction \(novel\)") of the same name. Eastwood plays Jonathan Hemlock in a role originally intended for [Paul Newman](/wiki/Paul_Newman "Paul Newman"), an assassin turned college art professor who decides to return to his former profession for one last "sanction" in return for a rare [Pissarro](/wiki/Pissarro "Pissarro") painting. In the process he must climb the north face of the [Eiger](/wiki/Eiger "Eiger") in Switzerland under perilous conditions. [Mike Hoover](/wiki/Mike_Hoover "Mike Hoover") taught Eastwood how to climb during several weeks of preparation at [Yosemite](/wiki/Yosemite "Yosemite") before filming commenced in [Grindelwald](/wiki/Grindelwald "Grindelwald"), Switzerland.[137][138] Despite prior warnings about the perils of the Eiger, Eastwood insisted on doing all his own climbing and stunts. The film crew suffered a number of accidents, including one fatality.[139][140] Eastwood cast former Bond girl [Claudine Auger](/wiki/Claudine_Auger "Claudine Auger") as his love interest, but deleted her entire performance from the movie.[141] Upon release in May 1975, _The Eiger Sanction_ was marginally successful commercially, receiving $14.2 million at the box-office, and gained mixed reviews.[142] Joy Gould Boyum of _[The Wall Street Journal](/wiki/The_Wall_Street_Journal "The Wall Street Journal")_ dismissed the film as "brutal fantasy".[142][143] Eastwood blamed Universal Studios for the film's poor promotion and turned his back on them to make an agreement with [Warner Bros.](/wiki/Warner_Bros. "Warner Bros."), through [Frank Wells](/wiki/Frank_Wells "Frank Wells"), that has lasted to the present day.[144]

[](/wiki/File:Josey_Wales_publicity.jpg)Chief Dan George with Locke and Eastwood at a barbecue in [Santa Fe, New Mexico](/wiki/Santa_Fe,_New_Mexico "Santa Fe, New Mexico"), promoting _The Outlaw Josey Wales_ (1976)

_[The Outlaw Josey Wales](/wiki/The_Outlaw_Josey_Wales "The Outlaw Josey Wales")_ (1976), a western inspired by [Asa Carter](/wiki/Asa_Carter "Asa Carter")'s 1972 novel of the same name,[145] has lead character Josey Wales (Eastwood) as a pro-Confederate guerrilla who refuses to surrender his arms after the American Civil War and is chased across the old southwest by a group of enforcers. The supporting cast included Locke as his love interest and [Chief Dan George](/wiki/Chief_Dan_George "Chief Dan George") as an elderly Cherokee who strikes up a friendship with Wales. Director [Philip Kaufman](/wiki/Philip_Kaufman "Philip Kaufman") was fired by producer Bob Daley at Eastwood's command in October 1975, three weeks into the shoot, resulting in a fine reported to be around $60,000 from the [Directors Guild of America](/wiki/Directors_Guild_of_America "Directors Guild of America") - who subsequently passed new legislation reserving the right to impose a major fine on a producer for discharging and replacing a director.[146] The film was pre-screened at the Sun Valley Center for the Arts and Humanities in Idaho during a six-day conference entitled _Western Movies: Myths and Images_. Invited to the screening were a number of well-known film critics, including Jay Cocks and Arthur Knight; directors such as [King Vidor](/wiki/King_Vidor "King Vidor"), [William Wyler](/wiki/William_Wyler "William Wyler"), and [Howard Hawks](/wiki/Howard_Hawks "Howard Hawks"); and a number of academics.[147] Upon its release in the summer of 1976, _The Outlaw Josey Wales_ was widely acclaimed, with many critics and viewers seeing Eastwood's role as an iconic one that related to America's ancestral past and the destiny of the nation after the American Civil War.[147] [Roger Ebert](/wiki/Roger_Ebert "Roger Ebert") compared the nature and vulnerability of Eastwood's portrayal of Josey Wales with his Man with No Name character in the _Dollars_ westerns, and praised the film's atmosphere.[148] The film would later appear in _Time_ 's "Top 10 Films of the Year".[149]

Eastwood was then offered the role of [Benjamin L. Willard](/wiki/Benjamin_L._Willard "Benjamin L. Willard") in [Francis Coppola](/wiki/Francis_Ford_Coppola "Francis Ford Coppola")'s _[Apocalypse Now](/wiki/Apocalypse_Now "Apocalypse Now")_ , but declined, for he did not wish to spend weeks on location in the Philippines.[150][151] He also refused the part of a platoon leader in [Ted Post](/wiki/Ted_Post "Ted Post")'s Vietnam War film _[Go Tell the Spartans](/wiki/Go_Tell_the_Spartans "Go Tell the Spartans")_ ,[150] and instead decided to make a third _Dirty Harry_ film, _[The Enforcer](/wiki/The_Enforcer_\(1976_film\) "The Enforcer \(1976 film\)")_ (1976). The film had Callahan partnered with a new female officer ([Tyne Daly](/wiki/Tyne_Daly "Tyne Daly")) to face a San Francisco Bay area group resembling the [Symbionese Liberation Army](/wiki/Symbionese_Liberation_Army "Symbionese Liberation Army"). The film, culminating in a shootout on [Alcatraz](/wiki/Alcatraz "Alcatraz") island, was considerably shorter than the previous _Dirty Harry_ films at 95 minutes,[152] but was a major commercial success grossing $100 million worldwide to become Eastwood's highest-grossing film to date.[153]

[](/wiki/File:Eastwood_Locke_The_Gauntlet_1977.jpg)Eastwood and Locke in _The Gauntlet_ (1977)

Eastwood directed and starred in _[The Gauntlet](/wiki/The_Gauntlet_\(film\) "The Gauntlet \(film\)")_ (1977) opposite Locke, Pat Hingle, [William Prince](/wiki/William_Prince_\(actor\) "William Prince \(actor\)"), [Bill McKinney](/wiki/Bill_McKinney "Bill McKinney"), and [Mara Corday](/wiki/Mara_Corday "Mara Corday"). In this film, he portrays a down-and-out cop assigned to escort a prostitute from Las Vegas to Phoenix to testify against the [mob](/wiki/Organized_crime "Organized crime"). Although a moderate hit with the viewing public, critics had mixed feelings about the film, with many believing it was overly violent. Ebert, in contrast, gave the film three stars and called it "classic Clint Eastwood: fast, furious, and funny".[154] In _[Every Which Way but Loose](/wiki/Every_Which_Way_but_Loose "Every Which Way but Loose")_ (1978), he had an uncharacteristic offbeat comedy role. His character, Philo Beddoe, is a trucker and brawler who roams the American West searching for a lost love (Locke) accompanied by his best friend, Orville Boggs (played by [Geoffrey Lewis](/wiki/Geoffrey_Lewis_\(actor\) "Geoffrey Lewis \(actor\)")) and an orangutan called Clyde. The film proved surprisingly successful upon its release and became Eastwood's most commercially successful film up to that time. Panned by critics, it ranked high among the box-office successes of his career and was the second-highest-grossing film of 1978.[155]

Eastwood starred in _[Escape from Alcatraz](/wiki/Escape_from_Alcatraz_\(film\) "Escape from Alcatraz \(film\)")_ (1979), the last of his films directed by Siegel. It was based on the true story of [Frank Lee Morris](/wiki/Frank_Morris_\(prisoner\) "Frank Morris \(prisoner\)") who, along with [John](/wiki/John_Anglin_\(criminal\) "John Anglin \(criminal\)") and [Clarence Anglin](/wiki/Clarence_Anglin "Clarence Anglin"), escaped from the notorious [Alcatraz Federal Penitentiary](/wiki/Alcatraz_Federal_Penitentiary "Alcatraz Federal Penitentiary") in 1962. The film was a major success; [Stanley Kauffmann](/wiki/Stanley_Kauffmann "Stanley Kauffmann") of _[The New Republic](/wiki/The_New_Republic "The New Republic")_ praised it as "crystalline cinema"[156] and Frank Rich of _Time_ described it as "cool, cinematic grace".[157]

[](/wiki/File:Any_Which_Way_You_Can.jpg)With Locke in _Any Which Way You Can_ (1980)

Eastwood directed and enacted the title role in _[Bronco Billy](/wiki/Bronco_Billy "Bronco Billy")_ (1980), alongside Locke, [Scatman Crothers](/wiki/Scatman_Crothers "Scatman Crothers"), and [Sam Bottoms](/wiki/Sam_Bottoms "Sam Bottoms").[158] Filming commenced on October 1, 1979, in the [Boise metropolitan area](/wiki/Boise_metropolitan_area "Boise metropolitan area") and was shot in five and a half weeks on a budget of $5 million.[143] Cinematographer [David Worth](/wiki/David_Worth_\(cinematographer\) "David Worth \(cinematographer\)") stated, "Being able to capture the true love between Clint and Sondra was very special."[159] Eastwood has cited _Bronco Billy_ as being one of the most relaxed shoots of his career and biographer [Richard Schickel](/wiki/Richard_Schickel "Richard Schickel") argued that Bronco Billy is Eastwood's most self-referential character.[160][161] The film was a commercial disappointment,[162] but was liked by critics. [Janet Maslin](/wiki/Janet_Maslin "Janet Maslin") of _The New York Times_ wrote that film was "the best and funniest Clint Eastwood movie in quite a while", and praised Eastwood's directing, intricately juxtaposing the old West and the new West.[163] Released later in 1980, _[Any Which Way You Can](/wiki/Any_Which_Way_You_Can "Any Which Way You Can")_ was the sequel to _Every Which Way but Loose_ and also starring Eastwood. The film received a number of bad reviews from critics, although Maslin described it as "funnier and even better than its predecessor".[162] In theaters over the Christmas season, _Any Which Way You Can_ was a major box office success and ranked among the year's top five highest-grossing films.[164]

[](/wiki/File:Eastwood1981.jpg)Eastwood in 1981

Eastwood directed and starred in _[Honkytonk Man](/wiki/Honkytonk_Man "Honkytonk Man")_ (1982), based on the eponymous [Clancy Carlile](/wiki/Clancy_Carlile "Clancy Carlile")'s depression-era novel. Eastwood portrays a struggling western singer Red Stovall who suffers from tuberculosis, but has finally been given an opportunity to make it big at the [Grand Ole Opry](/wiki/Grand_Ole_Opry "Grand Ole Opry"). He is accompanied by his young nephew (played by real-life son [Kyle](/wiki/Kyle_Eastwood "Kyle Eastwood")) to [Nashville, Tennessee](/wiki/Nashville,_Tennessee "Nashville, Tennessee"), where he is supposed to record a song. Only _Time_ gave the film a good review in the United States, with most reviewers criticizing its blend of muted humor and tragedy.[165] Nevertheless, the film received a more positive reception in France, where it was compared to [John Ford](/wiki/John_Ford "John Ford")'s _[The Grapes of Wrath](/wiki/The_Grapes_of_Wrath_\(film\) "The Grapes of Wrath \(film\)")_ ,[166] and it has since acquired the very high rating of 93 percent on Rotten Tomatoes.[167] Around the same time, Eastwood directed, produced, and starred in the [Cold War](/wiki/Cold_War "Cold War")-themed _[Firefox](/wiki/Firefox_\(film\) "Firefox \(film\)")_ (also 1982). Based on a 1977 [novel with the same name](/wiki/Firefox_\(novel\) "Firefox \(novel\)") written by [Craig Thomas](/wiki/Craig_Thomas_\(author\) "Craig Thomas \(author\)"), the film was shot before but released after _Honkytonk Man_. Russian filming locations were not possible due to the Cold War, and the film had to be shot in [Vienna](/wiki/Vienna "Vienna") and other locations in Austria to simulate many of the Eurasian story locations. With a production cost of $20 million, it was Eastwood's highest budget film to that time.[168] _[People](/wiki/People_\(American_magazine\) "People \(American magazine\)")_ likened Eastwood's performance to "[Luke Skywalker](/wiki/Luke_Skywalker "Luke Skywalker") trapped in Dirty Harry's Soul".[168]

Eastwood directed and starred in the fourth _Dirty Harry_ film, _[Sudden Impact](/wiki/Sudden_Impact "Sudden Impact")_ (1983), which is considered the darkest and most violent of the series.[169] By this time, Eastwood received 60 percent of all profits from films he starred in and directed, with the rest going to the studio.[170] _Sudden Impact_ was his final on-screen collaboration with Locke. She plays a middle-aged painter who, along with her sister, was gang-raped years before the story takes place and seeks revenge for her sister's now-vegetative state by systematically murdering the rapists. The line "[Go ahead, make my day](/wiki/Go_ahead,_make_my_day "Go ahead, make my day")" (uttered by Eastwood during an early scene in a coffee shop) has been cited as one of cinema's immortal lines. It was quoted by President [Ronald Reagan](/wiki/Ronald_Reagan "Ronald Reagan") in a speech to Congress, and used during the [1984 presidential elections](/wiki/1984_United_States_presidential_election "1984 United States presidential election").[171][172][173] The film was the second–most commercially successful of the _Dirty Harry_ films (after _The Enforcer_), earning $70 million. It received very positive reviews, with many critics praising the feminist aspects of the film through its explorations of the physical and psychological consequences of rape.[174]

_[Tightrope](/wiki/Tightrope_\(film\) "Tightrope \(film\)")_ (1984) had Eastwood starring opposite [Geneviève Bujold](/wiki/Genevi%C3%A8ve_Bujold "Geneviève Bujold") in a provocative thriller, inspired by newspaper articles about an elusive Bay Area rapist. Set in New Orleans to avoid confusion with the _Dirty Harry_ films,[175] Eastwood played a divorced cop drawn into his target's tortured psychology and fascination for [sadomasochism](/wiki/Sadomasochism "Sadomasochism").[176] _Tightrope_ was a critical and commercial hit and became the fourth highest-grossing [R-rated](/wiki/Motion_Picture_Association_of_America_film_rating_system "Motion Picture Association of America film rating system") film of 1984.[177] Eastwood next starred in the crime comedy _[City Heat](/wiki/City_Heat "City Heat")_ (also 1984) alongside [Burt Reynolds](/wiki/Burt_Reynolds "Burt Reynolds"), a film about an ex-cop turned private eye and his former police lieutenant partner who get mixed up with gangsters in the [Prohibition era](/wiki/Prohibition_era "Prohibition era") of the 1930s. The film grossed around $50 million domestically, but was overshadowed by [Eddie Murphy](/wiki/Eddie_Murphy "Eddie Murphy")'s _[Beverly Hills Cop](/wiki/Beverly_Hills_Cop "Beverly Hills Cop")_.[178]

[](/wiki/File:Clint_%26_Sondra_%26_Burt_%26_Loni.jpg)Eastwood and Locke at the premiere of _City Heat_ (1984) with Burt Reynolds and [Loni Anderson](/wiki/Loni_Anderson "Loni Anderson")

> Westerns. A period gone by, the pioneer, the loner operating by himself, without benefit of society. It usually has something to do with some sort of vengeance; he takes care of the vengeance himself, doesn't call the police. Like Robin Hood. It's the last masculine frontier. Romantic myth, I guess, though it's hard to think about anything romantic today. In a Western you can think, Jesus, there was a time when man was alone, on horseback, out there where man hasn't spoiled the land yet.

— Eastwood, on the philosophical allure of portraying western loners[179]

Eastwood made his only foray into TV direction with the _[Amazing Stories](/wiki/Amazing_Stories_\(1985_TV_series\) "Amazing Stories \(1985 TV series\)")_ episode "Vanessa in the Garden" (1985), which starred [Harvey Keitel](/wiki/Harvey_Keitel "Harvey Keitel") and Locke as a married couple. This was his first collaboration with [Steven Spielberg](/wiki/Steven_Spielberg "Steven Spielberg"), who later co-produced _[Flags of Our Fathers](/wiki/Flags_of_Our_Fathers_\(film\) "Flags of Our Fathers \(film\)")_ and _[Letters from Iwo Jima](/wiki/Letters_from_Iwo_Jima "Letters from Iwo Jima")_.[180] He would revisit the Western genre when he directed and starred in _[Pale Rider](/wiki/Pale_Rider "Pale Rider")_ (1985), a film based on the classic western _[Shane](/wiki/Shane_\(film\) "Shane \(film\)")_ (1953) and follows a preacher descending from the mists of the Sierras to side with the miners during the [California Gold Rush](/wiki/California_Gold_Rush "California Gold Rush") of 1850.[181] The title is a reference to the [Four Horsemen of the Apocalypse](/wiki/Four_Horsemen_of_the_Apocalypse "Four Horsemen of the Apocalypse"), as the rider of the pale horse is Death, and shows similarities to Eastwood's western _High Plains Drifter_ (1973) in its themes of morality and justice as well as its exploration of the supernatural.[182] It was hailed as one of the best films of 1985 and the best western to appear for a considerable period, with [Gene Siskel](/wiki/Gene_Siskel "Gene Siskel") of the _[Chicago Tribune](/wiki/Chicago_Tribune "Chicago Tribune")_ remarking, "This year (1985) will go down in film history as the moment Clint Eastwood finally earned respect as an artist."[183]

Eastwood co-starred with [Marsha Mason](/wiki/Marsha_Mason "Marsha Mason") in the military drama _[Heartbreak Ridge](/wiki/Heartbreak_Ridge "Heartbreak Ridge")_ (1986), about the 1983 United States [invasion of Grenada](/wiki/Invasion_of_Grenada "Invasion of Grenada"). He portrayed a United States Marine Corps [Gunnery Sergeant](/wiki/Gunnery_Sergeant "Gunnery Sergeant") veteran of the Vietnam War who realizes he is nearing the end of his military service. Production and filming were marred by internal disagreements between Eastwood and long-time friend and producer Fritz Manes, as well as between Eastwood and the [United States Department of Defense](/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Defense "United States Department of Defense"), which had expressed contempt for the film.[184][185] At the time, the film was a commercial rather than a critical success, and has only come to be viewed more favorably in recent times.[186] The film grossed $70 million domestically.[187]

Eastwood starred in _[The Dead Pool](/wiki/The_Dead_Pool "The Dead Pool")_ (1988), the fifth and final film in the _Dirty Harry_ series. It co-starred [Patricia Clarkson](/wiki/Patricia_Clarkson "Patricia Clarkson"), [Liam Neeson](/wiki/Liam_Neeson "Liam Neeson"), and a young [Jim Carrey](/wiki/Jim_Carrey "Jim Carrey") who plays Johnny Squares, a drug-addled rock star and the first of the victims on a list of celebrities drawn up by horror film director Peter Swan (Neeson) who are deemed most likely to die, the so-called "Dead Pool". The list is stolen by an obsessed fan who, in mimicking his favorite director, makes his way through the list killing off celebrities, of which Dirty Harry is also included. _The Dead Pool_ grossed nearly $38 million, relatively low receipts for a _Dirty Harry_ film. It is generally viewed as the weakest film of the series, though Roger Ebert thought it was as good as the original.[188][189]

Eastwood began working on smaller, more personal projects and experienced a lull in his career between 1988 and 1992. Always interested in jazz, he directed _[Bird](/wiki/Bird_\(1988_film\) "Bird \(1988 film\)")_ (1988), a biopic starring [Forest Whitaker](/wiki/Forest_Whitaker "Forest Whitaker") as jazz musician [Charlie "Bird" Parker](/wiki/Charlie_Parker "Charlie Parker"). Alto saxophonist [Jackie McLean](/wiki/Jackie_McLean "Jackie McLean") and [Spike Lee](/wiki/Spike_Lee "Spike Lee"), son of jazz bassist [Bill Lee](/wiki/Bill_Lee_\(musician\) "Bill Lee \(musician\)") and a long time critic of Eastwood, criticized the characterization of Charlie Parker remarking that it did not capture his true essence and sense of humor.[190] Eastwood received two [Golden Globes](/wiki/Golden_Globes "Golden Globes") for the film, the [Cecil B. DeMille Award](/wiki/Golden_Globe_Cecil_B._DeMille_Award "Golden Globe Cecil B. DeMille Award") for his lifelong contribution, and the [Best Director award](/wiki/Golden_Globe_Award_for_Best_Director "Golden Globe Award for Best Director"). However, _Bird_ was a commercial failure, earning just $11 million, which Eastwood attributed to the declining interest in jazz among black people.[191] Carrey would appear with Eastwood again in the poorly-received comedy _[Pink Cadillac](/wiki/Pink_Cadillac_\(film\) "Pink Cadillac \(film\)")_ (1989). The film is about a bounty hunter and a group of white supremacists chasing an innocent woman ([Bernadette Peters](/wiki/Bernadette_Peters "Bernadette Peters")) who tries to outrun everyone in her husband's prized pink [Cadillac](/wiki/Cadillac "Cadillac"). The film failed both critically and commercially,[192] earning barely more than _Bird_ and marking a low point in Eastwood's career.[193]

### 1990–2009: Critical acclaim and awards success

Eastwood directed and starred in _[White Hunter Black Heart](/wiki/White_Hunter_Black_Heart "White Hunter Black Heart")_ (1990), an adaptation of [Peter Viertel](/wiki/Peter_Viertel "Peter Viertel")'s _[roman à clef](/wiki/Roman_%C3%A0_clef "Roman à clef")_ , about [John Huston](/wiki/John_Huston "John Huston") and the making of the classic film _[The African Queen](/wiki/The_African_Queen_\(film\) "The African Queen \(film\)")_. Shot on location in Zimbabwe in the summer of 1989,[194] the film received some critical attention but with only a limited release earned just $8.4 million.[195] Eastwood directed and co-starred with [Charlie Sheen](/wiki/Charlie_Sheen "Charlie Sheen") in _[The Rookie](/wiki/The_Rookie_\(1990_film\) "The Rookie \(1990 film\)")_ , a buddy cop action film released in December 1990. Critics found the film's plot and characterization unconvincing, but praised its action sequences.[196] An ongoing lawsuit, in response to Eastwood allegedly ramming a woman's car,[197] resulted in no Eastwood films being shown in cinemas in 1991.[198] Eastwood won the suit and agreed to pay the complainant's legal fees if she did not appeal.[198]

> [I]f possible, he looks even taller, leaner and more mysteriously possessed than he did in Sergio Leone's seminal _Fistful of Dollars_ a quarter of a century ago. The years haven't softened him. They have given him the presence of some fierce force of nature, which may be why the landscapes of the mythic, late 19th-century West become him, never more so than in his new _Unforgiven_. ... This is his richest, most satisfying performance since the underrated, politically lunatic _Heartbreak Ridge_. There's no one like him.

— Vincent Canby of _The New York Times_ , on Eastwood's performance in _Unforgiven_[199]

Eastwood revisited the western genre in _[Unforgiven](/wiki/Unforgiven "Unforgiven")_ (1992), a film which he directed and starred in as an aging ex-gunfighter long past his prime. Scripts existed for the film as early as 1976 under titles such as _The Cut-Whore Killings_ and _The William Munny Killings_ , but Eastwood delayed the project because he wanted to wait until he was old enough to play his character and to savor it as the last of his western films.[198] _Unforgiven_ was a major commercial and critical success; Jack Methews of the _[Los Angeles Times](/wiki/Los_Angeles_Times "Los Angeles Times")_ described it as "the finest classical western to come along since perhaps John Ford's 1956 _[The Searchers](/wiki/The_Searchers_\(film\) "The Searchers \(film\)")_ ".[200] The film was nominated for nine [Academy Awards](/wiki/Academy_Award "Academy Award")[201] (including [Best Actor](/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Actor "Academy Award for Best Actor") for Eastwood and Best Original Screenplay for [David Webb Peoples](/wiki/David_Webb_Peoples "David Webb Peoples")), and won four, including [Best Picture](/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture "Academy Award for Best Picture") and [Best Director](/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Director "Academy Award for Best Director") for Eastwood. In 2008 _Unforgiven_ was ranked as the fourth-best American western, behind _[Shane](/wiki/Shane_\(film\) "Shane \(film\)")_ , _[High Noon](/wiki/High_Noon "High Noon")_ , and _The Searchers_ in the [American Film Institute](/wiki/American_Film_Institute "American Film Institute")'s "[AFI's 10 Top 10](/wiki/AFI%27s_10_Top_10 "AFI's 10 Top 10")" list.[202][203]

[](/wiki/File:Clint_Eastwood_Cannes_1993.jpg)Eastwood at the [1994 Cannes Film Festival](/wiki/1994_Cannes_Film_Festival "1994 Cannes Film Festival")

Eastwood played Frank Horrigan in the [Secret Service](/wiki/United_States_Secret_Service "United States Secret Service") thriller _[In the Line of Fire](/wiki/In_the_Line_of_Fire "In the Line of Fire")_ (1993), directed by [Wolfgang Petersen](/wiki/Wolfgang_Petersen "Wolfgang Petersen") and co-starring [John Malkovich](/wiki/John_Malkovich "John Malkovich") and [Rene Russo](/wiki/Rene_Russo "Rene Russo"). Horrigan is a guilt-ridden Secret Service agent haunted by his failure to save [John F. Kennedy](/wiki/John_F._Kennedy "John F. Kennedy")'s life.[204] The film was among the top 10 box office performers in that year, earning $102 million in the United States alone, and 25 years after he was first listed on Quigley's Top Ten Money Making Stars Poll, Eastwood was voted number one again.[205][72] A few months after film wrapped, Eastwood directed and co-starred alongside [Kevin Costner](/wiki/Kevin_Costner "Kevin Costner") in _[A Perfect World](/wiki/A_Perfect_World "A Perfect World")_ (also 1993). Set in the 1960s,[206] Eastwood plays a Texas Ranger in pursuit of an escaped convict (Costner) who hits the road with a young boy ([T.J. Lowther](/wiki/T.J._Lowther "T.J. Lowther")). Janet Maslin of _The New York Times_ wrote that the film marked the highest point of Eastwood's directing career,[207] and the film has since been cited as one of his most underrated directorial achievements.[208][209]

At the [1994 Cannes Film Festival](/wiki/1994_Cannes_Film_Festival "1994 Cannes Film Festival") Eastwood received France's [Ordre des Arts et des Lettres](/wiki/Ordre_des_Arts_et_des_Lettres "Ordre des Arts et des Lettres") medal,[210] and in 1995, he was awarded the [Irving G. Thalberg Memorial Award](/wiki/Irving_G._Thalberg_Memorial_Award "Irving G. Thalberg Memorial Award") at the [67th Academy Awards](/wiki/67th_Academy_Awards "67th Academy Awards").[211] His next film appearance was in a cameo role as himself in the children's film _[Casper](/wiki/Casper_\(film\) "Casper \(film\)")_ (1995). He expanded his repertoire by playing opposite [Meryl Streep](/wiki/Meryl_Streep "Meryl Streep") in _[The Bridges of Madison County](/wiki/The_Bridges_of_Madison_County_\(film\) "The Bridges of Madison County \(film\)")_ (also 1995). Based on the novel by [Robert James Waller](/wiki/Robert_James_Waller "Robert James Waller"),[212] the film relates the story of Robert Kincaid (Eastwood), a photographer working for _[National Geographic](/wiki/National_Geographic_\(magazine\) "National Geographic \(magazine\)")_ who, while photographing historic covered bridges in Iowa, meets and has an affair with an Italian-born farm wife, Francesca (Streep). Despite the novel receiving unfavorable reviews, _The Bridges of Madison County_ film was a commercial and critical success.[213] Roger Ebert wrote, "Streep and Eastwood weave a spell, and it is based on that particular knowledge of love and self that comes with middle age."[214] The film was nominated for a Golden Globe for [Best Motion Picture – Drama](/wiki/Golden_Globe_Award_for_Best_Motion_Picture_%E2%80%93_Drama "Golden Globe Award for Best Motion Picture – Drama") and won a [César Award](/wiki/C%C3%A9sar_Award "César Award") in France for Best Foreign Film. Streep was also nominated for an Academy Award and a Golden Globe. 

Eastwood directed and starred in the political thriller _[Absolute Power](/wiki/Absolute_Power_\(film\) "Absolute Power \(film\)")_ (1997), alongside [Gene Hackman](/wiki/Gene_Hackman "Gene Hackman") (with whom he had appeared in _Unforgiven_). Eastwood played the role of a veteran thief who witnesses the Secret Service cover-up of a murder. The film received a mixed reception from critics.[215] Later in 1997, Eastwood directed _[Midnight in the Garden of Good and Evil](/wiki/Midnight_in_the_Garden_of_Good_and_Evil_\(film\) "Midnight in the Garden of Good and Evil \(film\)")_ , based on the novel by [John Berendt](/wiki/John_Berendt "John Berendt") and starring [John Cusack](/wiki/John_Cusack "John Cusack"), [Kevin Spacey](/wiki/Kevin_Spacey "Kevin Spacey"), and [Jude Law](/wiki/Jude_Law "Jude Law"). The film met with a mixed critical response.[216]

> The roles that Eastwood has played, and the films that he has directed, cannot be disentangled from the nature of the American culture of the last quarter century, its fantasies and its realities.

— Author Edward Gallafent, commenting on Eastwood's impact on film from the 1970s to 1990s[217]

Eastwood directed and starred in _[True Crime](/wiki/True_Crime_\(1999_film\) "True Crime \(1999 film\)")_ (1999). He plays Steve Everett, a journalist and recovering alcoholic, who has to cover the execution of murderer Frank Beechum (played by [Isaiah Washington](/wiki/Isaiah_Washington "Isaiah Washington")). _True Crime_ received a mixed reception, with Janet Maslin of _The New York Times_ writing, "his direction is galvanized by a sense of second chances and tragic misunderstandings, and by contrasting a larger sense of justice with the peculiar minutiae of crime. Perhaps he goes a shade too far in the latter direction, though."[218] The film was a box office failure, earning less than half its $55 million budget and was Eastwood's worst-performing film of the 1990s aside from _White Hunter Black Heart_ , which had a limited release.[219]

Eastwood directed and starred in _[Space Cowboys](/wiki/Space_Cowboys "Space Cowboys")_ (2000) alongside [Tommy Lee Jones](/wiki/Tommy_Lee_Jones "Tommy Lee Jones"), Donald Sutherland and [James Garner](/wiki/James_Garner "James Garner"). Eastwood played one of a group of veteran ex-test pilots sent into space to repair an old Soviet satellite. The original music score was composed by Eastwood and [Lennie Niehaus](/wiki/Lennie_Niehaus "Lennie Niehaus"). _Space Cowboys_ was critically well-received and holds a 79 percent rating at Rotten Tomatoes,[220] although Roger Ebert wrote that the film was, "too secure within its traditional story structure to make much seem at risk".[221] The film grossed more than $90 million in its United States release, more than Eastwood's two previous films combined.[222] Eastwood played an ex-[FBI](/wiki/Federal_Bureau_of_Investigation "Federal Bureau of Investigation") agent chasing a sadistic killer ([Jeff Daniels](/wiki/Jeff_Daniels "Jeff Daniels")) in the thriller _[Blood Work](/wiki/Blood_Work_\(film\) "Blood Work \(film\)")_ (2002), loosely based on the 1998 [novel of the same name](/wiki/Blood_Work_\(novel\) "Blood Work \(novel\)") by [Michael Connelly](/wiki/Michael_Connelly "Michael Connelly"). The film was a commercial failure, grossing just $26.2 million on an estimated budget of $50 million and received mixed reviews, with Rotten Tomatoes describing it as, "well-made but marred by lethargic pacing".[223]

[](/wiki/File:AngelinaJolieClintEastwoodCroppedMay08.jpg)With a pregnant Angelina Jolie on the red carpet of the [2008 Cannes Film Festival](/wiki/2008_Cannes_Film_Festival "2008 Cannes Film Festival")

Eastwood directed and scored the crime drama _[Mystic River](/wiki/Mystic_River_\(film\) "Mystic River \(film\)")_ (2003), a film dealing with themes of murder, vigilantism and sexual abuse and starring [Sean Penn](/wiki/Sean_Penn "Sean Penn"), [Kevin Bacon](/wiki/Kevin_Bacon "Kevin Bacon"), and [Tim Robbins](/wiki/Tim_Robbins "Tim Robbins"). The film was praised by critics and won two Academy Awards – Best Actor for Penn and Best Supporting Actor for Robbins – with Eastwood garnering nominations for Best Director and Best Picture.[224] The film grossed $90 million domestically on a budget of $30 million.[225] In 2003, Eastwood was named Best Director of the Year by the [National Society of Film Critics](/wiki/National_Society_of_Film_Critics "National Society of Film Critics").[226]

> Clint is a true artist in every respect. Despite his years of being at the top of his game and the legendary movies he has made, he always made us feel comfortable and valued on the set, treating us as equals.

— Tim Robbins, on working with Eastwood.[11]

The following year, Eastwood found further critical acclaim with _[Million Dollar Baby](/wiki/Million_Dollar_Baby "Million Dollar Baby")_. The boxing drama won four Academy Awards for Best Picture, Best Director, Best Actress ([Hilary Swank](/wiki/Hilary_Swank "Hilary Swank")) and Best Supporting Actor ([Morgan Freeman](/wiki/Morgan_Freeman "Morgan Freeman")).[227] At age 74, Eastwood became the oldest of eighteen directors to have directed two or more Best Picture winners.[228][229] He also received a nomination for Best Actor, as well as a [Grammy](/wiki/Grammy "Grammy") nomination for his score,[230] and won a Golden Globe for Best Director, which was presented to him by daughter Kathryn, who was [Miss Golden Globe](/wiki/Miss_Golden_Globe "Miss Golden Globe") at the [2005 ceremony](/wiki/62nd_Golden_Globe_Awards "62nd Golden Globe Awards").[231] [A. O. Scott](/wiki/A._O._Scott "A. O. Scott") of _The New York Times_ lauded the film as a "masterpiece" and the best film of the year.[232]

Eastwood directed two films about World War II's [Battle of Iwo Jima](/wiki/Battle_of_Iwo_Jima "Battle of Iwo Jima") released in 2006. The first, [_Flags of Our Fathers_](/wiki/Flags_of_Our_Fathers_\(film\) "Flags of Our Fathers \(film\)"), focused on the men who raised the American flag on top of [Mount Suribachi](/wiki/Mount_Suribachi "Mount Suribachi") and featured the film debut of Eastwood's son [Scott](/wiki/Scott_Eastwood "Scott Eastwood"). This was followed by _[Letters from Iwo Jima](/wiki/Letters_from_Iwo_Jima "Letters from Iwo Jima")_ , which dealt with the tactics of the Japanese soldiers on the island and the letters they wrote home to family members. _Letters from Iwo Jima_ was the first American film to depict a war issue completely from the view of an American enemy.[233] Both films received praise from critics and garnered several nominations at the [79th Academy Awards](/wiki/79th_Academy_Awards "79th Academy Awards"), including Best Director, Best Picture, and [Best Original Screenplay](/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Original_Screenplay "Academy Award for Best Original Screenplay") for _Letters from Iwo Jima_. At the [64th Golden Globe Awards](/wiki/64th_Golden_Globe_Awards "64th Golden Globe Awards") Eastwood received nominations for Best Director in both films. _Letters from Iwo Jima_ won the award for [Best Foreign Language Film](/wiki/Golden_Globe_Award_for_Best_Foreign_Language_Film "Golden Globe Award for Best Foreign Language Film").[234]

[](/wiki/File:ClintEastwoodCannesMay08.jpg)Eastwood in 2008

Eastwood next directed _[Changeling](/wiki/Changeling_\(film\) "Changeling \(film\)")_ (2008), based on a true story set in the late 1920s. [Angelina Jolie](/wiki/Angelina_Jolie "Angelina Jolie") stars as a woman reunited with her missing son only to realize he is an impostor.[235] After its release at several film festivals the film grossed over $110 million, the majority of which came from foreign markets.[236] The film was highly acclaimed, with Damon Wise of _[Empire](/wiki/Empire_\(magazine\) "Empire \(magazine\)")_ describing _Changeling_ as "flawless".[237] [Todd McCarthy](/wiki/Todd_McCarthy "Todd McCarthy") of _[Variety](/wiki/Variety_\(magazine\) "Variety \(magazine\)")_ described it as "emotionally powerful and stylistically sure-handed" and that the film's characters and social commentary were brought into the story with an "almost breathtaking deliberation".[238] For the film, Eastwood received nominations for [Best Original Score](/wiki/Golden_Globe_Award_for_Best_Original_Score "Golden Globe Award for Best Original Score") at the [66th Golden Globe Awards](/wiki/66th_Golden_Globe_Awards "66th Golden Globe Awards"), [Best Direction](/wiki/BAFTA_Award_for_Best_Direction "BAFTA Award for Best Direction") at the [62nd British Academy Film Awards](/wiki/62nd_British_Academy_Film_Awards "62nd British Academy Film Awards") and director of the year from the London Film Critics' Circle.[239][240]

Eastwood ended a four-year "self-imposed acting hiatus"[241] by appearing in _[Gran Torino](/wiki/Gran_Torino "Gran Torino")_ (also 2008), which he also directed, produced and partly scored with his son Kyle and [Jamie Cullum](/wiki/Jamie_Cullum "Jamie Cullum"). Biographer Marc Eliot called Eastwood's role "an amalgam of the Man with No Name, Dirty Harry, and [William Munny](/wiki/William_Munny "William Munny"), here aged and cynical but willing and able to fight on whenever the need arose".[242] _Gran Torino_ grossed almost $30 million during its opening weekend release in January 2009, the highest of his career as an actor or director.[243] _Gran Torino_ eventually grossed over $268 million in theaters worldwide, becoming the highest-grossing film of Eastwood's career so far (without adjustment for inflation).[244]

Eastwood's 30th directorial outing came with _[Invictus](/wiki/Invictus_\(film\) "Invictus \(film\)")_ (2009), a film based on the story of the [South African team](/wiki/South_Africa_national_rugby_union_team "South Africa national rugby union team") at the [1995 Rugby World Cup](/wiki/1995_Rugby_World_Cup "1995 Rugby World Cup") with [Morgan Freeman](/wiki/Morgan_Freeman "Morgan Freeman") as [Nelson Mandela](/wiki/Nelson_Mandela "Nelson Mandela"), [Matt Damon](/wiki/Matt_Damon "Matt Damon") as rugby team captain [François Pienaar](/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Pienaar "François Pienaar"), and [Grant L. Roberts](/wiki/Grant_L._Roberts "Grant L. Roberts") as [Ruben Kruger](/wiki/Ruben_Kruger "Ruben Kruger").[245] The film was met with generally positive reviews; Roger Ebert gave it three and a half stars and described it as a "very good film ... with moments evoking great emotion",[246] while _Variety_ 's Todd McCarthy wrote, "Inspirational on the face of it, Clint Eastwood's film has a predictable trajectory, but every scene brims with surprising details that accumulate into a rich fabric of history, cultural impressions and emotion."[247] For the film, Eastwood was nominated for Best Director at the [67th Golden Globe Awards](/wiki/67th_Golden_Globe_Awards "67th Golden Globe Awards").[239]

### 2010–present: Directorial focus and later roles

In the Eastwood-directed _[Hereafter](/wiki/Hereafter_\(film\) "Hereafter \(film\)")_ (2010), he again worked with Matt Damon, who portrayed a psychic. The film had its world premiere at the [Toronto International Film Festival](/wiki/2010_Toronto_International_Film_Festival "2010 Toronto International Film Festival") and was given a limited theatrical release.[248][249] _Hereafter_ received mixed reviews from critics, with the consensus at Rotten Tomatoes being, "Despite a thought-provoking premise and Clint Eastwood's typical flair as director, _Hereafter_ fails to generate much compelling drama, straddling the line between poignant sentimentality and hokey tedium."[250] Around the same time, Eastwood served as executive producer for a [TCM](/wiki/Turner_Classic_Movies "Turner Classic Movies") documentary about jazz pianist [Dave Brubeck](/wiki/Dave_Brubeck "Dave Brubeck"), _[Dave Brubeck: In His Own Sweet Way](/wiki/Dave_Brubeck:_In_His_Own_Sweet_Way "Dave Brubeck: In His Own Sweet Way")_ (also 2010), to commemorate Brubeck's 90th birthday.[251]

[](/wiki/File:Clint_Eastwood_J._Edgar_Premier,_November_2011_\(cropped\).jpg)Eastwood at the Washington, D.C. premiere of _J. Edgar_ (2011)

Eastwood directed _[J. Edgar](/wiki/J._Edgar "J. Edgar")_ (2011), a biopic of [FBI director](/wiki/Director_of_the_Federal_Bureau_of_Investigation "Director of the Federal Bureau of Investigation") [J. Edgar Hoover](/wiki/J._Edgar_Hoover "J. Edgar Hoover"), with [Leonardo DiCaprio](/wiki/Leonardo_DiCaprio "Leonardo DiCaprio") in the title role.[252] The film received mixed reviews, although DiCaprio's performance as Hoover was widely praised.[253] Roger Ebert wrote that the film is "fascinating", "masterful", and praised DiCaprio's performance.[254] [David Edelstein](/wiki/David_Edelstein "David Edelstein") of _[New York](/wiki/New_York_\(magazine\) "New York \(magazine\)")_ magazine, while also praising DiCaprio, wrote, "It's too bad _J. Edgar_ is so shapeless and turgid and ham-handed, so rich in bad lines and worse readings."[255] Eastwood starred in the baseball drama _[Trouble with the Curve](/wiki/Trouble_with_the_Curve "Trouble with the Curve")_ (2012), as a veteran baseball scout who travels with his daughter for a final scouting trip. [Robert Lorenz](/wiki/Robert_Lorenz "Robert Lorenz"), who worked with Eastwood as an assistant director on several films, directed the film.[256]

> Everybody wonders why I continue working at this stage. I keep working because there's always new stories. ... And as long as people want me to tell them, I'll be there doing them.

— Eastwood, reflecting on his later career[257]

During [Super Bowl XLVI](/wiki/Super_Bowl_XLVI "Super Bowl XLVI"), Eastwood narrated a halftime advertisement for [Chrysler](/wiki/Chrysler "Chrysler") titled "[Halftime in America](/wiki/Halftime_in_America "Halftime in America")" (2012).[258] The advertisement was criticized by several U.S. Republicans, who claimed it implied that President [Barack Obama](/wiki/Barack_Obama "Barack Obama") deserved a second term.[259] In response to the criticism, Eastwood stated, "I am certainly not politically affiliated with Mr. Obama. It was meant to be a message about job growth and the spirit of America."[260]

Eastwood next directed _[Jersey Boys](/wiki/Jersey_Boys_\(film\) "Jersey Boys \(film\)")_ (2014), a musical biopic based on the [Tony Award](/wiki/Tony_Award "Tony Award")-winning [musical of the same name](/wiki/Jersey_Boys "Jersey Boys").
